# Visual prompt engineering for video models

Robert Geirhos<sup>\*1</sup>, Yuxuan Li<sup>\*1</sup>, Thaddäus Wiedemer<sup>\*1</sup>, Neha Kalibhat<sup>1</sup>, Zi Wang<sup>1</sup>, Mani Malek<sup>1</sup>,   
Oyvind Tafjord<sup>1</sup>, Kevin Swersky<sup>1</sup>, Been Kim<sup>1</sup> and Priyank Jaini<sup>\*1</sup>   
<sup>1</sup>Goo<sub>g</sub>le Dee<sub>p</sub>Mind

In the age of foundation models, a model is only as good as its prompt. For this reason, prompt engineering has become an essential technique for improving language model performance. Since video models are currently becoming foundation models for visual tasks (e.g., visual reasoning), we here ask whether they similarly benefit from visual prompt engineering: automatically modifying the task image to improve model performance. For example, for a visual physics reasoning task (“Where does the ball land, after passing a set of obstacles?”), an abstract sketch-like scene can be turned into a photorealistic version with a simple call to an image editing model. We find that visual prompt engineering, or VIPE for short, improves video reasoning performance across tasks. In fact, for video models, visual prompt engineering can be even more efective than classic text-based prompt engineering or test-time scaling. Ultimately, just as text-based prompt engineering systematically improves language model performance, visual prompt engineering can serve as a simple, compute-eficient approach to elicit better visual reasoning performance from video models. Example videos on our project page.

We address this gap by asking: Can we improve visual reasoning capabilities of video models by transforming the input task image? We call this visual prompt engineering, or VIPE. Concretely, VIPE transforms the visual <sub>p</sub>rom<sub>p</sub>t (e.<sub>g</sub>., re<sub>p</sub>lacin<sub>g</sub> an abstract ball-and-ram<sub>p</sub> sketch with a <sub>p</sub>hotorealistic scene of billiard balls rollin<sub>g</sub> down wooden shelves) usin<sub>g</sub> an ima<sub>g</sub>e editin<sub>g</sub> model, and then <sub>p</sub>asses the transformed ima<sub>g</sub>e to the video model for reasonin<sub>g</sub> (e.<sub>g</sub>., <sub>g</sub>eneratin<sub>g</sub> a visual <sub>p</sub>rediction for where the ball would roll). The underl<sub>y</sub>in<sub>g</sub> task remains identical; onl<sub>y</sub> its visual <sub>p</sub>resentation chan<sub>g</sub>es, with th<sub>e</sub> <sub>goa</sub>l <sub>o</sub>f <sub>prov</sub>idi<sub>ng</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>v</sub>i<sub>sua</sub>l <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ex</sub>t th<sub>a</sub>t th<sub>e</sub> <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l <sub>can</sub> <sub>reason</sub> <sub>over</sub> <sub>more</sub> <sub>e</sub>f<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub>l<sub>y.</sub>

A video model attem<sub>p</sub>ts to solve each task sam<sub>p</sub>le b<sub>y</sub> <sub>g</sub>eneratin<sub>g</sub> a video. ## 3. Text-based prompt engineering helps language models for language tasks. Does visual prompt engineering help video models for visual tasks?

We start our investigation with visual reasoning on the VPCT [31] dataset. It requires predicting whether a white ball will dro<sub>p</sub> into buckets 1, 2, or 3 after rollin<sub>g</sub> down a series of ram<sub>p</sub>s (re<sub>p</sub>resented as black lines). Sam<sub>p</sub>les are shown in Fi<sub>g</sub>. 2. The dataset is interestin<sub>g</sub> because it re<sub>q</sub>uires models to <sub>reason</sub> th<sub>roug</sub>h <sub>a</sub> <sub>c</sub>h<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>eng</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>v</sub>i<sub>sua</sub>l <sub>se</sub>t<sub>up</sub> <sub>w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> t<sub>a</sub>ki<sub>ng</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o</sub> <sub>accoun</sub>t <sub>w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>p</sub>h<sub>ys</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l f<sub>orces</sub> <sub>app</sub>l<sub>y.</sub> At th<sub>e same</sub> ti<sub>me,</sub> th<sub>e pro</sub>bl<sub>em can</sub> b<sub>e represen</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> it<sub>s or</sub>i<sub>g</sub>i<sub>na</sub>l <sub>s</sub>k<sub>e</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>-s</sub>t<sub>y</sub>l<sub>e se</sub>t<sub>up, or a</sub>lt<sub>erna</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub>l<sub>y</sub> i<sub>n a</sub> <sub>p</sub>h<sub>o</sub>t<sub>orea</sub>li<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>c way.</sub> W<sub>e</sub> h<sub>ere as</sub>k <sub>w</sub>h<sub>e</sub>th<sub>er</sub> th<sub>ese c</sub>h<sub>o</sub>i<sub>ces</sub> i<sub>n</sub>fl<sub>uence</sub> h<sub>ow we</sub>ll <sub>v</sub>id<sub>eo mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s per</sub>f<sub>orm on</sub> thi<sub>s</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k<sub>.</sub> T<sub>o</sub> thi<sub>s en</sub>d<sub>, we</sub> t<sub>a</sub>k<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e or</sub>i<sub>g</sub>i<sub>na</sub>l <sub>s</sub>k<sub>e</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t <sub>an</sub>d <sub>per</sub>f<sub>orm v</sub>i<sub>sua</sub>l <sub>promp</sub>t <sub>eng</sub>i<sub>neer</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> (VIPE) to make it look <sub>p</sub>hotorealistic. Until recentl<sub>y</sub>, chan<sub>g</sub>in<sub>g</sub> the st<sub>y</sub>le of an entire dataset would h<sub>ave</sub> b<sub>een pro</sub>hibiti<sub>ve</sub>l<sub>y expens</sub>i<sub>ve, requ</sub>i<sub>r</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> d<sub>ays o</sub>f <sub>wor</sub>k b<sub>y a pro</sub>f<sub>ess</sub>i<sub>ona</sub>l<sub>.</sub> F<sub>or</sub>t<sub>una</sub>t<sub>e</sub>l<sub>y, we can now</sub> simply preprocess the dataset with an image editing model. For VPCT, we instantiate the editor � with Nano Banana Pro [32] and <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>ose an edit instruction $t _ { \mathrm { e d i t } }$ ourselves: “Transform this into a realistic photograph, without changing the camera perspective that is facing the wall dead on. Replace the white circle with a small red billiard ball. Replace each black line by a wooden shelf board of exactly the same length, orientation, and placement. ## 4. How can visual prompt engineering be automated?

The VPCT results from Sec. 3 demonstrate that a good visual prompt can systematically improve video <sub>reason</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>per</sub>f<sub>ormance.</sub> H<sub>owever,</sub> <sub>we</sub> <sub>may</sub> <sub>no</sub>t <sub>a</sub>l<sub>ways</sub> h<sub>ave</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>goo</sub>d <sub>pr</sub>i<sub>or</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>u</sub>iti<sub>on</sub> <sub>on</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>a</sub>t <sub>cons</sub>tit<sub>u</sub>t<sub>es</sub> a <sub>g</sub>ood <sub>p</sub>rom<sub>p</sub>t<sub>,</sub> and the visual <sub>p</sub>rom<sub>p</sub>t search s<sub>p</sub>ace is vast. Here<sub>,</sub> we therefore test whether VIPE can be automated b<sub>y</sub> com<sub>p</sub>arin<sub>g</sub> two a<sub>pp</sub>roaches, freeform ideation and ste<sub>p</sub>-b<sub>y</sub>-ste<sub>p</sub> edits (ACE). T<sub>o</sub> b<sub>e</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ear:</sub> <sub>our</sub> <sub>ma</sub>i<sub>n</sub> <sub>con</sub>t<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e</sub> i<sub>ns</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>v</sub>id<sub>eo</sub> <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l <sub>reason</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> i<sub>mproves</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>v</sub>i<sub>sua</sub>l prompt engineering (VIPE). How VIPE is instantiated is up to the community, will likely change as <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s ge</sub>t b<sub>e</sub>tt<sub>er, an</sub>d <sub>mos</sub>t i<sub>mpor</sub>t<sub>an</sub>tl<sub>y,</sub> b<sub>u</sub>ild<sub>s on s</sub>t<sub>an</sub>d<sub>ar</sub>d<sub>, es</sub>t<sub>a</sub>bli<sub>s</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d <sub>componen</sub>t<sub>s.</sub> W<sub>e regar</sub>d th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>ac</sub>t th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>ex</sub>i<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>ng mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s an</sub>d <sub>me</sub>th<sub>o</sub>d<sub>s can</sub> b<sub>e p</sub>l<sub>ugge</sub>d i<sub>n as an a</sub>d<sub>van</sub>t<sub>age, an</sub>d <sub>o</sub>f <sub>course</sub> d<sub>on</sub>’t <sub>c</sub>l<sub>a</sub>i<sub>m</sub> <sub>any nove</sub>lt<sub>y regar</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>ese ex</sub>i<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> t<sub>ec</sub>h<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ques.</sub> Si<sub>nce v</sub>id<sub>eo genera</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> i<sub>s curren</sub>tl<sub>y expens</sub>i<sub>ve, we</sub> <sub>cons</sub>i<sub>s</sub>t<sub>en</sub>tl<sub>y per</sub>f<sub>orm a</sub>ll <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>o</sub>ll<sub>ow</sub>i<sub>ng exper</sub>i<sub>men</sub>t<sub>s w</sub>ith V<sub>eo</sub> 3<sub>.</sub>1<sub>.</sub>

Performance is plotted as error reduction (in percent) relative to the baseline of no visual <sub>p</sub>rom<sub>p</sub>t en<sub>g</sub>ineerin<sub>g</sub>. For exam<sub>p</sub>le<sub>,</sub> if the baseline achieves 20% accurac<sub>y</sub> and VIPE achieves 60% accurac<sub>y</sub>, the error rate is halved (80% errors → 40% errors), resultin<sub>g</sub> in a 50% reduction. ## 4.1. Freeform ideation by a VLM

We start with a naive a<sub>pp</sub>roach: <sub>g</sub>eneratin<sub>g</sub> <sub>p</sub>rom<sub>p</sub>t en<sub>g</sub>ineerin<sub>g</sub> ideas $t _ { \mathrm { e d i t } }$ <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>a</sub> VLM id<sub>ea</sub>t<sub>or.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> VLM’s <sub>g</sub>eneral ideation <sub>p</sub>rom<sub>p</sub>t $t _ { \mathrm { i d e a t e } }$ <sub>see</sub>k<sub>s</sub> t<sub>o</sub> t<sub>rans</sub>f<sub>er</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k t<sub>o a new v</sub>i<sub>sua</sub>l d<sub>oma</sub>i<sub>n w</sub>hil<sub>e preserv</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> spatial layout and object correspondence. In practice, we found the resulting image edits to be faithful t<sub>o</sub> thi<sub>s</sub> i<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>ti<sub>on, on</sub>l<sub>y c</sub>h<sub>ang</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e v</sub>i<sub>sua</sub>l <sub>appearance o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> i<sub>mages w</sub>ith<sub>ou</sub>t <sub>a</sub>lt<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k<sub>.</sub> Here, we instantiate the VIPE ideator with Gemini 3.1 Pro [33], the ima<sub>g</sub>e editor as Nano Banana 2 (Gemini 3.1 Flash Ima<sub>g</sub>e), and the filterin<sub>g</sub> model a<sub>g</sub>ain as Gemini 3.1 Pro (selectin<sub>g</sub> the best out of 5 attem<sub>p</sub>ts). The ideator does not receive feedback from downstream evaluation, i.e., o<sub>p</sub>erates <sub>open-</sub>l<sub>oop.</sub> F<sub>u</sub>ll <sub>promp</sub>t<sub>s an</sub>d d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>a</sub>il<sub>s are prov</sub>id<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> S<sub>ec.</sub> E<sub>.</sub>1<sub>.</sub> Th<sub>e resu</sub>lt<sub>s are s</sub>h<sub>own</sub> i<sub>n</sub> Fi<sub>g.</sub> 3<sub>.</sub> A<sub>cross a</sub> <sub>w</sub>id<sub>e range o</sub>f t<sub>as</sub>k<sub>s, we s</sub>h<sub>ow</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t thi<sub>s s</sub>i<sub>mp</sub>l<sub>e</sub> f<sub>ree</sub>f<sub>orm, open-</sub>l<sub>oop v</sub>i<sub>sua</sub>l <sub>promp</sub>t <sub>eng</sub>i<sub>neer</sub>i<sub>ng approac</sub>h <sub>success</sub>f<sub>u</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> fi<sub>n</sub>d<sub>s many a</sub>lt<sub>erna</sub>ti<sub>ve v</sub>i<sub>sua</sub>l <sub>promp</sub>t<sub>s</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>e</sub>li<sub>c</sub>it b<sub>e</sub>tt<sub>er per</sub>f<sub>ormance</sub> i<sub>n</sub> V<sub>eo</sub> 3<sub>.</sub>1<sub>.</sub> S<sub>ome</sub> task variants allow Veo 3.1 to achieve sur<sub>p</sub>risin<sub>g</sub>l<sub>y</sub> lar<sub>g</sub>e <sub>p</sub>erformance <sub>g</sub>ains (reducin<sub>g</sub> the error rate b<sub>y</sub> over 75% com<sub>p</sub>ared to the baseline of no visual <sub>p</sub>rom<sub>p</sub>t en<sub>g</sub>ineerin<sub>g</sub>), sim<sub>p</sub>l<sub>y</sub> b<sub>y</sub> alterin<sub>g</sub> the visual <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ex</sub>t i<sub>n w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h th<sub>e mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l’<sub>s reason</sub>i<sub>ng occurs.</sub>

## 4.2. Step-by-step edits: Atomic Concept Editing (ACE)

A<sub>n a</sub>lt<sub>erna</sub>ti<sub>ve, more s</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>ure</sub>d <sub>approac</sub>h f<sub>or v</sub>i<sub>sua</sub>l <sub>promp</sub>t <sub>eng</sub>i<sub>neer</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> i<sub>s e</sub>diti<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e or</sub>i<sub>g</sub>i<sub>na</sub>l i<sub>mage</sub> ste<sub>p</sub>-b<sub>y</sub>-ste<sub>p</sub>, one conce<sub>p</sub>t at a time (e.<sub>g</sub>., ori<sub>g</sub>inal ima<sub>g</sub>e → chan<sub>g</sub>e back<sub>g</sub>round to blue → increase object size → ...). To this end, we ada<sub>p</sub>t the ACE framework [37] to the visual <sub>p</sub>rom<sub>p</sub>t en<sub>g</sub>ineerin<sub>g</sub> settin<sub>g</sub>. Visual vs. text prompt engineering Results are shown in Fig. 6. Overall, we find that both text <sub>an</sub>d <sub>v</sub>i<sub>sua</sub>l <sub>promp</sub>t <sub>eng</sub>i<sub>neer</sub>i<sub>ng su</sub>b<sub>s</sub>t<sub>an</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> i<sub>mprove per</sub>f<sub>ormance over</sub> th<sub>e</sub> b<sub>ase</sub>li<sub>ne across</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k<sub>s,</sub> <sub>con</sub>fi<sub>rm</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>eac</sub>h <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>a</sub>lit<sub>y</sub> i<sub>s a v</sub>i<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> l<sub>ever</sub> f<sub>or e</sub>li<sub>c</sub>iti<sub>ng</sub> b<sub>e</sub>tt<sub>er v</sub>id<sub>eo reason</sub>i<sub>ng.</sub> F<sub>or examp</sub>l<sub>e, on</sub> Sort 3 Numbers, the best text variant and the best image variant each achieved roughly a 20-fold improvement (86% and 76%, vs. 4% without prompt engineering). ## Appendix

## A. Datasets

VPCT MIT license, dataset on HuggingFace by camelCase12 [31], 100 samples. For the results i<sub>n</sub> S<sub>ec.</sub> 4<sub>, we use</sub> th<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>rs</sub>t t<sub>en samp</sub>l<sub>es.</sub> F<sub>or</sub> S<sub>ec.</sub> 3<sub>,</sub> th<sub>e en</sub>ti<sub>re</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t i<sub>s use</sub>d<sub>.</sub>

Conjunctive Search, Sort 3 Numbers, Connect the Dots Task ideas were drawn with permission from the authors of Wiedemer et al. [9]. Datasets were <sub>g</sub>enerated manuall<sub>y</sub> in Ke<sub>y</sub>note b<sub>y</sub> the authors. Conjunctive Search contains 5 image samples, each showing 2 blue circles (targets), 4 red circles (distractors) and 4 blue squares (distractors) at diferent positions. Sort 3 Numbers contains 5 image samples of diferent sets of double-digit numbers. Connect the Dots contains 6 image samples of 3 <sub>same-co</sub>l<sub>ore</sub>d <sub>c</sub>i<sub>rc</sub>l<sub>es</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>vary</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>co</sub>l<sub>ors.</sub>

Maze With permission from the authors, we use the four maze solving splits from Wiedemer et al. [9]. The first three s<sub>p</sub>lits with 50 sam<sub>p</sub>les each are mazes on s<sub>q</sub>uare <sub>g</sub>rids with size 5 × 5, 7 × 7, and 9 × 9. The last s<sub>p</sub>lits contains 40 non-axis-ali<sub>g</sub>ned mazes with curved <sub>p</sub>aths. We re<sub>p</sub>ort results on the fi<sub>rs</sub>t t<sub>en samp</sub>l<sub>es o</sub>f <sub>eac</sub>h <sub>sp</sub>lit i<sub>n</sub> S<sub>ec.</sub> 4<sub>.</sub>

RushHour One task from the MentisOculi benchmark [13], published under an Apache 2.0 license. Figure 8 | Control conditions reveal shallow physics understanding of vision-language models. Prompt: “Predict which bucket (numbered 1, 2, or 3 from left to right) the ball will eventually fall into. If the ball will not fall into any bucket, output ‘answer(0)’; if the ball falls into a bucket, output ‘answer(X)’ where X is the bucket number (1, 2, or 3).’ Original (a): VLMs appear to understand the setup and correctly predict the bucket. Control condition upside-down buckets (b): The model still believes that the ball will drop into a bucket, revealing a shallow understanding. Control condition with realistic upside-down buckets (c): In order to rule out that the VLM has, in fact, a <sub>g</sub>ood understandin<sub>g</sub> of <sub>p</sub>h<sub>y</sub>sics and just misunderstands the abstract sketch setu<sub>p</sub> of condition (b), a <sub>rea</sub>li<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>c con</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>l <sub>con</sub>diti<sub>on con</sub>fi<sub>rms</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t th<sub>e mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l’<sub>s un</sub>d<sub>ers</sub>t<sub>an</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> i<sub>s</sub> i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>ee</sub>d <sub>s</sub>h<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>ow:</sub> it <sub>s</sub>till d<sub>oes no</sub>t <sub>ge</sub>t th<sub>e answer r</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht<sub>.</sub>

The Wan model has the o<sub>p</sub>tion enable/disable the <sub>p</sub>rom<sub>p</sub>t rewriter (Qwen2.5-Plus). The white circle must fall, interact rigidly with the drawn lines, and settle into one of the bottom containers. Animate this cross-section: the white boulder drops, rolls down the slanted black cliff ledges under gravity, and settles into one of the three valley basins below. Animate this side-view transparent coin box. The white round coin falls, rolling and sliding naturally down the slanted lines. Apply accurate rigid-body gravity and collision physics until it drops into one of the three bottom</td></tr></table>

Animate the same-colored circles as liquid drops. For each matching pair, have them organically expand outward until they touch and fuse together into a single fluid shape. Sequentially spawn a glowing spark at one circle and animate it traveling to the other circle of the exact same color. The spark must disappear upon arrival, leaving no trail.</td></tr><tr><td>pair until they turn solid gray, then proceed to the next pair.</td><td></td><td></td><td>Sequentially select each pair of same-colored circles. ## E.2. Step-by-step edits: ACE

In this section, we <sub>p</sub>rovide more details for Sec. 4.2. We a<sub>pp</sub>l<sub>y</sub> the atomic conce<sub>p</sub>t editin<sub>g</sub> (ACE) framework [37] to both text variant and image variant settings. ## ACE tree search configuration

The exploration tree has three depth levels with branching factors of (6, 5, 3) at depths 0, 1, and 2, res<sub>p</sub>ectivel<sub>y</sub>. At each node, one video is <sub>g</sub>enerated <sub>p</sub>er reference ima<sub>g</sub>e (three videos total), and the autorater scores all of them. The objective is considered satisfied for a node if the mean of its autorater scores exceeds the minimum score threshold. Nodes whose objective is already satisfied are not ex<sub>p</sub>anded further (i.e., no child mutations are <sub>g</sub>enerated from successful nodes).

## F. Test-time scaling details

We add test-time scaling budget allocation results for Maze and RushHour similar to Fig. 5 in Figs. 18 and 19. Since self-consistenc<sub>y</sub> (majorit<sub>y</sub> votin<sub>g</sub>) is not strai<sub>g</sub>ht-forward for these tasks (outcomes are not simple choices but trajectories/sequences), we report pass rate as a proxy instead. ## G. Image model comparison

Thi<sub>s sec</sub>ti<sub>on con</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>ns a more</sub> d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>a</sub>il<sub>e</sub>d di<sub>scuss</sub>i<sub>on o</sub>f th<sub>e resu</sub>lt<sub>s</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> S<sub>ec.</sub> 7<sub>.</sub>

VIPE provides diminished or negative benefit for image generation models For NB Pro in image mode, VIPE <sub>y</sub>ields onl<sub>y</sub> a modest +5 <sub>pp</sub> im<sub>p</sub>rovement in Pass@1 over raw sketches (50% vs. 45%). Thi<sub>s</sub> i<sub>s</sub> f<sub>ar sma</sub>ll<sub>er</sub> th<sub>an</sub> th<sub>e</sub> +18 <sub>pp</sub> i<sub>mprovemen</sub>t th<sub>a</sub>t VIPE <sub>prov</sub>id<sub>es</sub> f<sub>or</sub> V<sub>eo</sub> 3<sub>.</sub>1 <sub>on</sub> th<sub>e same</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k (Fi<sub>g</sub>. 2). More strikin<sub>g</sub>l<sub>y</sub>, for NB Flash in ima<sub>g</sub>e mode, VIPE hurts; Pass@1 dro<sub>p</sub>s from 37% (sketch) to 21% (VIPE), a −16 <sub>pp</sub> de<sub>g</sub>radation. Majorit<sub>y</sub> vote accurac<sub>y</sub> tells a similar stor<sub>y</sub>, fallin<sub>g</sub> from 48% to 32%. Wh<sub>y</sub> does this ha<sub>pp</sub>en?

The image generation pathway is a bottleneck The comparison between NB Flash in image mode and text mode is especially revealing, since these use the exact same underlying model. In text mode, th<sub>e mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l <sub>s</sub>i<sub>mp</sub>l<sub>y ou</sub>t<sub>pu</sub>t<sub>s a</sub> l<sub>a</sub>b<sub>e</sub>l<sub>;</sub> i<sub>n</sub> i<sub>mage mo</sub>d<sub>e,</sub> it <sub>mus</sub>t <sub>genera</sub>t<sub>e an</sub> i<sub>mage</sub> d<sub>ep</sub>i<sub>c</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e</sub> b<sub>a</sub>ll’<sub>s</sub> final <sub>p</sub>osition. On VIPE in<sub>p</sub>uts, switchin<sub>g</sub> from ima<sub>g</sub>e to text mode im<sub>p</sub>roves Pass@1 from 21% to 50% (+29 <sub>pp</sub>). On sketch in<sub>p</sub>uts, the im<sub>p</sub>rovement is +21 <sub>pp</sub> (37% → 58%). ## H. Autoraters

E<sub>ac</sub>h <sub>v</sub>id<sub>eo</sub> i<sub>s score</sub>d b<sub>ase</sub>d <sub>on a</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k<sub>-spec</sub>ifi<sub>c</sub> d<sub>e</sub>fi<sub>n</sub>iti<sub>on o</sub>f <sub>success,</sub> th<sub>e eng</sub>i<sub>neere</sub>d i<sub>mage promp</sub>t<sub>, an</sub>d sample-specific information such as initial object positions. For VLM-based autoraters, the rubric has to be adjusted to fit the final image prompt variant, e.g., to mention specific appearances of various objects of interest. In this case, we instruct an LLM to adjust a base rubric based on the ideator output. Maze, Connect the Dots, Sort 3 Numbers, Conjunctive Search, and VPCT use Gemini 3.1 Pro as an autorater. RushHour uses an algorithmic autorater.

## H.4. RushHour autoraters

Solutions in RushHour are not necessarily unique (in some samples, obstacle cars can be moved inde<sub>p</sub>endentl<sub>y</sub> in various orders). The task as formulated b<sub>y</sub> Zeller et al. [13] also does not <sub>p</sub>enalize non-o<sub>p</sub>timal solutions that, e.<sub>g</sub>., move cars back-and-forth or (unnecessaril<sub>y</sub>) move distractor cars. Fortunatel<sub>y</sub>, the dataset comes with a validator that determines whether a <sub>g</sub>iven move se<sub>q</sub>uence (e.<sub>g</sub>., Car A forward, Car B backward, Car R forward) so<sup>l</sup>ves a puzz<sup>l</sup>e. [13]), and <sub>p</sub>rovides absolute movement directions on an <sub>e</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht<sub>-po</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t <sub>compass rose, w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>we can map</sub> t<sub>o</sub> f<sub>orwar</sub>d/b<sub>ac</sub>k<sub>war</sub>d b<sub>ase</sub>d <sub>on eac</sub>h <sub>car</sub>’<sub>s pre</sub>d<sub>e</sub>fi<sub>ne</sub>d <sub>ax</sub>i<sub>s</sub> <sub>o</sub>f <sub>movemen</sub>t<sub>.</sub> Thi<sub>s promp</sub>t<sub>,</sub> t<sub>oo, con</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>ns p</sub>l<sub>ace</sub>h<sub>o</sub>ld<sub>ers popu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>w</sub>ith th<sub>e appropr</sub>i<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> t<sub>erms</sub> f<sub>or</sub> th<sub>e</sub> puzz<sup>l</sup>e var<sup>i</sup>ant, e.g., us<sup>i</sup>ng anodized metal slides/deadbolts, metal slider, puzzle box backplate, an<sup>d</sup> brass exit slot <sup>f</sup>or objects, object\_singular, container, an<sup>d</sup> exit <sup>f</sup>or t<sup>h</sup>e anodized\_metal\_puzzle\_box var<sup>i</sup>ant. <sup>R</sup>us<sup>hH</sup>ou<sup>r</sup> seque<sup>n</sup>ce pa<sup>r</sup>s<sup>in</sup>g p<sup>r</sup>o<sup>m</sup>p<sup>t</sup>

[video]

Y<sub>ou are an exper</sub>t <sub>v</sub>id<sub>eo ana</sub>l<sub>yzer.</sub> A<sub>na</sub>l<sub>yze</sub> th<sub>e v</sub>id<sub>eo w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>s</sub>h<sub>ows a puzz</sub>l<sub>e</sub> b<sub>e</sub>i<sub>ng so</sub>l<sub>ve</sub>d<sub>.</sub> Th<sub>e puzz</sub>l<sub>e</sub> i<sub>s a var</sub>i<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on o</sub>f RushHour. In this variation:

• The objects are {objects}.

• The board/container is {container}.

• The exit is {exit}.

• One of the objects is the target (usually red/pinkish, labeled ‘R’ or ‘red\_car’), which needs to reach the {exit}. Your task is to extract the sequence of moves of the {objects} from the video and evaluate if the video remains <sub>va</sub>lid<sub>.</sub> O<sub>n</sub>l<sub>y</sub> <sub>cons</sub>id<sub>er</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>par</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> <sub>v</sub>id<sub>eo</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>e</sub> b<sub>eg</sub>i<sub>nn</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>un</sub>til th<sub>e</sub> <sub>scene</sub> b<sub>ecomes</sub> i<sub>nva</sub>lid<sub>.</sub> A <sub>scene</sub> b<sub>ecomes</sub> i<sub>nva</sub>lid if<sub>:</sub>

• The layout of the {container} changes.

• {objects} appear or disappear.

• {objects} morph, deform, or change color.

• {objects} leave their defined tracks/axes.
